ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e2664	We're coming to the 1st. Question No 9, by Mr Dr. Einm to Mr Bundeskanzler Please, honourable Member.
ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e2682	Mr Bundeskanzler, my question: 9/M “What are the essential elements and content that should be included in the European Constitutional Treaty?”
ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e2704	Please, Mr. Federal Chancellor.
ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e2719	Ladies and gentlemen! Ladies and gentlemen! As you all know, a large majority of the Member States of the European Union have ratified this Treaty, namely, 18 Member States. I do not really think it is good that the dynamics in the current debate is almost exclusively based on the fact that some states have not signed and they are trying to water it down in different areas now. I have full understanding of countries such as France and the Netherlands, where there has been a referendum with a No, because there is clearly a need for action. But that States that do not have no no in a referendum which has signed still have to negotiate changes afterwards, I believe, not the best European spirit. What is the point of the negotiations now under way? . I believe that a routing of the institutional part would be a big mistake, because that was a historic compromise that was found here if you wanted to. I think we would get into a very difficult situation by routing this institutional package. As regards the rest of the Treaty, the principle applies to Austria: The content is more important than the form. So, of course, it is important to us that the Charter of Fundamental Rights is legally binding; in what form it is legally binding, one can talk about it. In our opinion, however, there is also a close link between the compromise that was reached in the institutional part and, above all, the third section on the issue of a number of policy objectives and tasks for the European Union, which we all consider very important together. The Spring Summit has shown that the issue of climate change is of pan-European importance and that this should, among other things, be a fundamental task for the European Union, similar to that of the common energy policy and a number of other issues. Therefore, we want to work within the European Council to maintain the uniform legal personality of the European Union, to abolish the three-pillar structure, to maintain the new system of competence, to maintain the position of national parliaments in the control of the principle of subsidiarity, to ensure that there is an improved legal protection before the ECJ, and to maintain the social rules set out in the Constitutional Treaty – that is, I believe, important for the development of the social dimension. In addition, we will be strongly committed to the goal of full employment and to the anchoring of general care at this Council conference.

I also have the impression that the intention of the new French President Sarkozy is to be helpful in the formulation of this clear mandate, but we must see that there are a number of countries that have very serious concerns because they have partly completely different integration policy objectives or priorities. You know that the UK has proposed major changes, that there are also approaches in the Czech Republic and in Poland, which are actually talking about a Europe other than that laid down in the Constitutional Treaty. We have agreed, even at an informal meeting with President-in-Office Angela Merkel, that we are also trying to convince those countries in which there are major problems bilaterally. I will discuss this question with the Polish Prime Minister Kaczyński next Monday. I therefore hope that we will succeed in formulating this clear mandate in June, because this is the essential basis for meeting the timetable for a renewed basis for the European Union by 2009.

ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e2926	Additional question? – Mr Scheibner, please.
ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e2944	Federal Chancellor! On the question of the European Constitution, we always hear from all experts what is not to come. I think it would be interesting to consider: How do you get out of this problem? From an Austrian point of view, would it not be useful to support a new idea, such as a kind, as we suggested by the BZÖ, of creating core Europe, i.e. different levels of integration, which the countries choose for themselves by means of referendums?
ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e2959	Federal Chancellor, please.
ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e2975	This idea of a core Europe has been a long way off. You remember that at that time, Mr. Lamers, the spokesperson of the CDU, Mr. Lamers, had already raised this question more than a decade ago, even before the question of Austrian accession to the European Union was raised. There have always been different concepts. What has prevailed in reality has to be said quite openly, is a Europe of different speeds, because we have, so to speak, the core area of the European Union, and beyond, we have Schengen and the euro. There are a number of Member States that are members of the euro area and there are a number of Member States that are members of Schengen. There are Member States with both members, there are Member States that are only with one of the two Member States, and there are Member States which are neither member. That is to say, we have already achieved a situation of very different levels of integration in the European Union. This Constitutional Treaty is, or has been, an attempt to merge all the Treaties that have existed so far into a Treaty and, of course, to extend it by a number of points. The main task was to maintain the functioning of the European Union on the expanded basis of 27. I have the impression that there will probably be another contract technology now, namely a return to the old contract technology, namely that not a new treaty will replace all existing ones, but that the new Treaty will contain a number of proposals to amend the existing Treaties and that if one wants to do so, the classical structure of the European Union will be less modified than it would have been provided for in the Constitutional Treaty. I think it is important for Austria that we always be there when the European Union moves into a new level of integration, because – and this is important – a small country in the heart of Europe is strong only if it is part of the strongest part of Europe. Therefore, we are committed to a very pro-European integration policy because we believe that this also strengthens the Austrian position. I believe that the question of core Europe is not a realistic option because of these different speeds in the current situation.

Constitutions are not “octroyed”, but constitutions are prepared, decided and then ratified by the relevant bodies – that is in the Austrian democracy of the National Council, with a two-thirds majority. Therefore, it is perfectly clear: Of course, if there were to be any significant change in the Treaty already ratified, a ratification procedure would have to take place again in Austria, as in all other countries. [[Abg. Strache: No referendum?]] I assume that the new Treaty will not go beyond the previous Treaty – otherwise, I would say a little miracle – but it is more likely that we will have to do everything we can to preserve the substance of the existing Treaty. And if the old Treaty does not give rise to the need for a referendum, it will not result in the new Treaty either. But I very much regret that the basis was not created for what would have been the most meaningful thing, namely a basis for a pan-European vote on this Constitutional Treaty, because that would have been the very democratic way and would have saved us a lot in the past.

ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e3787	Mr Bundeskanzler, my question: 2/M “When will the Federal Government finally tackle the necessary total reform of the Eco electricity Act in the face of the threat of climate change?”
ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e3809	Federal Chancellor, please.
ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e3824	Ladies and gentlemen! Climate change is a topic of major importance, which will also be discussed at the G8 summit this week. I hope that the results will be sufficient to achieve a global change of course here. We must also be aware of the extent to which the European contribution is taking place, in the face of all the concerns of this issue. Europe is currently contributing a 14 percent share of global CO2 emissions, which will fall to 10 percent by 2020. That means, even if we are very committed to Europe – for which I am referring – it must be clear that this will not save the world, but that we need a global effort to deal with this climate change. It is therefore important, above all, to reach an agreement on the basis of a credible European basis also with the other most important countries, namely China, India, the United States of America, Brazil, Russia and others, otherwise our great effort will be relatively small. The second is the dimension of Austria – Austria is one of the committed countries in the European Union as regards climate protection – because we have two dimensions to consider: One thing. is the reduction of CO2 emissions, and the other is the expansion of renewable energies. I would like to put the following thought into the space: that, if we do what we are doing here, of course, we have to ask ourselves the question of what economic means use we achieve what extent of CO2 reduction. After all, all consumers have to pay this over the electricity price. Here we note that there are, of course, huge differences in the cost efficiency of these individual models. To give just one example: For example, you know that the greatest efficiency with regard to CO2 savings has thermal building renovation. Here, with a relatively low input, you have a maximum CO2 emission savings. On the other hand, there are very expensive methods – unfortunately also biomass recycling in individual areas, which is quite expensive – as regards the reduction of CO2 emissions. In the middle there is an entire range of possibilities: Wind power plants, of course also bioheating plants, which have a cheaper price. What we're talking about is: If, following the climate summit, we are thinking about a new basis for the Eco electricity Act, then, I believe, it will not only be a matter of promoting individual forms of renewable energy, but above all, it will be a matter of improving energy efficiency – similar to that in the German Ecostrom Act – because the German Ecostrom Act is not only an ecostrom, but also an energy efficiency law. On the basis of these two fundamental considerations, the competent Federal Ministers will negotiate this over the next few weeks and put on the table a new proposal to the public, NGOs and stakeholders. It is true that at the Federal Government's Climate Change Summit, the existing eco electricity law has been subject to a very strong criticism and we have said that we are willing to think about what changes we can aim for here.

As you know, only 26 percent of the total CO2 emissions in Austria come from transport; and as far as transport is concerned, this climate and energy funds will not be the right contact point, but will ultimately be the new road cost directive of the European Union, which is an extension of rail infrastructure, an appropriate alternative. to have available, and in the end this is also a comprehensive transport policy. In other words, this climate and energy fund is an essential instrument in an area where we have not yet worked to the extent and is therefore an important complement to existing measures. But this alone will not solve the issues of global warming and CO2 problems, but we will have to work with the same commitment to renewable energy, to transport policy and also to spatial planning policy if we want to be sustainablely successful.

ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e4030	Federal Chancellor! Small eco-electric plants, i.e. biogas, small hydropower plants and photovoltaics, create value added in the region. ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e4056	Please, Mr. Federal Chancellor.
ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e4072	Ladies and gentlemen, the point is that the price situation is important to us, but of course we also take into account the additional effects of the development of the habitat. The problem we face is that in Austria, as regards renewable energy, we have already used our capacities relatively much. To tell you what sizes are: We currently have 23 percent renewable energy in Austria. If one, so to speak, without taking into account what is politically enforceable or not, exploits all the possibilities of wind power in Austria, this is a potential of about 5 percent, but this does not mean that one can do it, because there are, of course, all possible connections where windwheels cannot be installed. The second is: If we were to exploit all the resources available through hydropower, which are theoretically existing in Austria, this would require an additional capacity of around 7 percent, including all waters. ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e4177	Please, Mr. Federal Chancellor.
ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e4192	Ladies and gentlemen! France, under the former President of Chirac, has tried to bring nuclear energy to renewable energy, which I felt was a completely absurd process; this discussion has also succeeded in preventing this. But what is undoubtedly right – and this cannot be doubted – is that the use of nuclear energy contributes to the reduction of CO2 emissions, which in turn says nothing about the other existing risks, which are still present, namely both the safety risks of the plants and the unexplained question of the final storage. Therefore, nuclear energy remains a highly dangerous technology, even if it will have a positive effect on CO2 emissions. I note that there is unfortunately a renaissance of nuclear energy policy in the European Union. There are many countries that are already using nuclear energy, which are now expanding. There are states that actually wanted to leave, but it is noted that they are moving the time of the exit ever further backwards. It is also noted that some countries with no nuclear energy use so far are already thinking about using nuclear energy. This is, of course, a debate that is primarily conducted against the background of security of supply and the background of Europe's very strong dependence, especially on the energy sources of Russia and the Middle East. I believe that this is not a reasonable strategy in the long term, because it is necessary to see that the basis for nuclear energy – completely away from all the safety risks – comes to a large extent from the same countries. More than 50 percent of the uranium as the basis of nuclear energy production also comes from Russia – so this is not really a change, for example, in relation to the natural gas or oil situation. I therefore believe that Austria should consistently maintain its previous line, but of course it means that in the other areas, renewable energy et cetera, we are much more, much more demanding than the other countries might be.

This means, in practice, that schools, kindergartens and other care facilities themselves are now obliged to report to youth welfare providers on suspicions. These resources could then be missing in other tasks, but they are also indispensable. Then: It was boring, I think, for young people's well-being to date. These concerns were raised in the Council of Ministers, and a protocol note from the Minister of Education stated that the Ministry of Family Affairs will actively and directly monitor and serve the schools and, in particular, will inform them of what matters are subject to reporting and which are not in order to avoid the best possible reporting errors. This Protocol also states that this cooperation is a special form of assistance and that schools do not in any way become executives of youth welfare. This must, of course, also apply to all other non-school institutions. We have discussed this amendment very intensively in committee, and this is a right which we are not allowed to agree on as a government group. A discussion must also be conducted intensively by government groups. It was also important to me in the Committee that the Minister said that if the new reporting requirements increase in resource requirements, they will also be increased according to federal funds. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! Given the overall problem, this innovation is really a very small step. The big throw is certainly not yet. All the youth welfare, the federal law, as well as the state laws, belong to their modernity and practicality reflected and reviewed. We urgently need uniform standards at the highest level. Perhaps we also understand a better concept than the term “youth welfare” which appears quite antique. But, of course, it is not a question of terms, but, as a whole, it is not about increasing the resources for youth welfare in general. Education aids, family aid must not fail in money, and accommodation for third-country nationals must really be the highest quality standards. Our children just have to be worth it. And if we do not recognize this value, we will have to pay a very high price for it.

ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e6606	Next, Mrs Mag. Muttons. 3 minutes welcome. Please, ladies and gentlemen.
ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e6628	Madam President. Mrs. Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! The amendment to the Youth Welfare Act aims to ensure a better flow of information between the competent authorities and educational and care facilities. The aim is therefore to identify and act as quickly as possible on neglects and other risks of children. At present, authorities mainly have a reporting obligation in the field of youth welfare law, but in the future these reporting obligations should be more applied to schools and care facilities. I think this is a sensible measure, because it is precisely there that problems of children are most often perceived and known. Teachers and teachers, educators and educators usually have the opportunity to perceive signs of problems or also of impairments relatively early. The early warning system that we want to decide with this novel is therefore positive, but a whole series of questions remain open, which will be resolved in the near future. As the first important step, the immediate network needs to be narrower, narrower. The students' counsellors who are specifically trained for this purpose, often also referred to as educational advisers, are strengthened by improved and additional training; in recent years, much has been cut down and reduced. School psychology is unhealingly overloaded and under-occupied, even here there is a need for action. But there are other questions, such as: What are the criteria for assessing persons active in the care facilities? Or: Who exactly is required to report? What specific facts should be reported? And it is – and this has already been mentioned – the question of resources, because high numbers of cases and increasingly scarce resources in the youth offices often limit the scope for action. We need more down-to-earth social work, more community prevention models, because this would also greatly facilitate prevention in youth welfare. It is also very important that children in all federal states have the same standards. We still have nine national laws on the protection of young people. This includes actions committed. So there's still a lot to do. Nevertheless, this novelle is a first real step. - Thank you.

Health insurance is only transferred to vaccination with the vaccine, but not to the supply. ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e11283	The next item is the speech by Mrs Mag. Dr. Karl with 2 minutes of voluntary speaking time limit. Please, ladies and gentlemen.
ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e11304	Madam President. Appreciate members of the government! High house! My contribution to the debate refers to Amendment No 31/A, to that for women who are 60 years old. completed and raised at least one child who is required to introduce monthly support. In addition to the above-mentioned conditions of claim, Austrian citizenship is required as a further condition. To this end, the following are to be explained: The grant of the aid requires that no income or only social assistance under the compensatory allowance rate is obtained. Support is therefore granted in relation to needs. This benefit is to be financed from the general means of taxation. It follows that this support is a social assistance measure. However, Article 24 of the EU Citizens Directive states that EU Member States may refuse to provide EU citizens with social assistance only for the first three months of their stay. In the event of a longer stay, the host Member State shall provide social assistance to EU citizens. That is to say, a restriction on the the application for support to Austrian citizens cannot be compatible with the EU Citizens' Directive. However, there is also the requirement that the applicant should not live in the common budget with a person liable for maintenance. This condition leads to very contradictory results. On the basis of my short speaking time, however, I would no longer like to address these contradictory results, but would like to conclude by referring to Mrs Belakovich-Jenewein's speech, which refers to two very essential principles of Austrian social security law. One of the fundamental principles of social security is the principle of solidarity. But solidarity also means that we have a solid balance between those who earn little and those who achieve a high income. A second principle of our social security system is the so-called territoriality principle. That is, we do not rely on citizenship, but rather we insist that the place of employment is located in the country. I see no reason why we should differ from these two principles only because of polemic speeches. - Thank you.

Point Report of the Committee on Labour and Social Affairs on the Government Template (52 e.g._Other Organiser Federal Law amending the Civilian Vacation and Processing Act as well as the Construction Workers' Bad Weather Compensation Act 1957 (118 d.b.)
ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e14708	We're on the 10th. Agenda item. There was no oral reporting. That is what we are getting to the debate. First of all, Mr Muchitsch, has been mentioned. 4 minutes welcome. Please, honourable Member.
ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e14738	Madam President. Mr. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! I would also like to recall the historical development briefly, because I believe it is of great importance to this novel. The holiday scheme, the core area of the Civilian Vacation and Processing Act, is already going back to a collective contractual arrangement before the Second World War. In 1946, the first strong sign of the Austrian social partners was the building workers' leave fund as a public law body in the self-management of the social partners. In 1987 – a century law – construction workers were also created, the first industry-handling system. The administration was transferred to its own administrative bodies within the building workers' holiday and handling fund. In the 1990s, the building workers' holiday and handling fund was transferred to the management of the bad weather scheme and created its own subject area for the winter holiday scheme. The power volume of the building workers' holiday and handling cash can also be seen. In the field of holidays alone, benefits of more than € 700 million are provided in the year. Last year, the processing services under the old processing right have reached €62 million, the services under the construction worker bad weather compensation law €35 million and the services for the winter holidays €32 million. In addition, in 2002, the Baubeiter-Urlaubungs- und Abhandlungskasse established its own employee pension fund for workers' claims, and it manages a collective-contractual shift leave scheme for construction workers in shift operations. Since September 2005, workers who are not Austrian citizens but who are sent or left to Austria have also been included in the holiday scheme of the Building Workers' Vacation and Processing Act. This is important for social policy, but also very important for equal competition among companies, among construction companies. My Dear Ones In short, ladies and gentlemen: It is the task of the building workers' holiday and handling fund, including construction workers who are safe to work for a particularly sensitive industry, to secure a variety of social policy claims. The common responsibility of the social partners in the construction sector and those responsible for politics is then to shape the structures of this institution in such a way that they have grown in responsibilities. I come to the four issues of this intelligible novel. Firstly, the summary of the administrative bodies. The amendment summarises administrative bodies for the holiday scheme and the settlement scheme. This will achieve a streamlining of the structures. Secondly, the establishment of organisational units at regional level. Here too, it is planned that only with a two-thirds majority will the national authorities and in the case of the Committee decide how regional support will look in the future. Thirdly, the extension of the access possibilities of the construction workers' holiday and handling fund to the data of the social security institutions, which is also very important. Finally, the last area concerns a temporary re-regulation of the German Federal Government's liability for the subject area of bad weather compensation for the building workers' holiday and handling fund. This provision should avoid an otherwise threatening increase in the contribution of workers and employers and at the same time create pressure on the social partners to take measures to reduce the financial burden on labour market policy. We must not – and this is also our responsibility as a legal representative – leave the construction workers and, above all, the buildings of the building area alone in the rain, but we must also take additional measures to improve annual employment and employment throughout the year by means of policy and thus improve the labour market situation in the construction sector. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen, after very emotional issues which have been discussed before, I would like to conclude by expressing my thanks to all the political groups who have decided unanimously in the Committee on the recommendation to the National Council, and to conclude with the request to the National Council that the amendment be approved today. – Thank you for your attention.

Point Report of the Committee on Justice on the Government Template (48 e.g._Other Organiser Federal Law amending the Federal Law on judicial cooperation in criminal matters with the Member States of the European Union (EU-JZG) (EU-JZG-Eng 2007) (135 d.a.)
ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e18926	We're going to the 15th. Agenda item. There was no oral reporting. I would ask the first speaker to speak. This is Dr. Jarolim, Member of Parliament, with a three-minute voluntary time limit. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2007-06-06-023-XXIII-NRSITZ-00025_d8e18953	Madam President. Mrs. Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! I would ask you, Madam President, to stop speaking for 2 minutes, because I have divided it with colleague Ablinger, so I have to take a short look. This is about the implementation of a Council decision on the subject of harmonising in Europe the fines imposed by a court, by an authority, by a public prosecutor. We all know that a common Europe, a common rule of law, of course, also needs harmonisation in the field of justice and enforcement of decisions. There has already been a wide-ranging discussion in the field of administrative law, such as the collection of fines due to excessive speed in road transport. Today we are dealing with judicial decisions, public prosecutor's decisions to be recognised. One of the key issues is: Where should a state not be forced to recognize decisions by another? – This is the question in particular: Where is there such differences within the legal standards that it is not reasonable? The Council Decision also provides for such exceptions – and they have also been incorporated. For example: Penalties up to €70 will not be executed. Or: If the perpetrator in a country is sentenced to the law of that country at the time of the offence, the question arises: Where is this judgment to be executed? So if a person commits a crime in France and is sentenced there, this sentence will be executed in Austria. However, if it has not yet been punished under Austrian law, the judgment will not be executed. If there is an amnesty or a grace in another state, the judgment is not executed either. Finally, the judgment is not enforced in the case of non-dispute judgments. There are also convictions, punishments for sex, race, religion, ethnicity, nationality. Ladies and gentlemen! We are on the way to making effective legal application and enforcement of standards in a common Europe. This is a step forward. It's not a spectacular step. It's, I mean, rather a Of course. Nevertheless, it is necessary that we implement them here. - Thank you.
